  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.1, F33.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I’m just… tired all the time. And I know that sounds vague, because I’m a teacher, so of course I’m tired at the end of the day, everyone at school is tired. But this feels different. It’s not just “long day with the kids” tired.\n\nEven when I get a full night’s sleep, I wake up and I already feel like I’m pushing through mud. Then after school I still have grading, lesson prep, dinner, getting things ready for the next morning, and it all feels like I have to force myself through every single step. I can do it, mostly, but it takes so much effort. I guess I’m here because it’s been wearing me down, and I don’t know if this is something I’m supposed to just manage or if something’s wrong.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're still enjoying the things you used to enjoy, like teaching or activities outside of school?\n\nPatient: I still enjoy some of it, I think… or at least I care about it. I still show up for my students, and I’m not checked out in the classroom exactly. I can still have moments where a kid finally understands something, or says something funny, and I feel that little spark of why I became a teacher. So it’s not like everything is completely flat.\n\nBut I’ve definitely pulled back from the parts of teaching that used to make me feel more like myself. I used to mentor newer teachers, help with extra projects, make these more creative classroom activities just because I wanted to. Lately I’m doing what needs to be done, but not much beyond that. If someone asks me to join a committee or help plan something, my first thought is just, “I can’t add one more thing.”\n\nOutside of school it’s kind of the same. I still do what I need to for my family, but I don’t really say yes to social things much anymore. I used to meet friends after work or do little creative routines at home that made my life feel fuller, and now I mostly just… don’t. I tell myself I’m too busy or too tired, and sometimes that’s true, but I know I’ve been withdrawing. It feels like I’m still functioning, but my life has gotten smaller.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most days, I think. I wouldn’t say I’m crying all the time or obviously miserable from the outside, because I’m usually still able to smile for my students and get through the lessons and be “on” when I need to be. In the classroom, I can sort of step into that role, and I don’t want the kids to feel it from me.\n\nBut underneath that, there’s this gray, flat feeling that’s just… there. It follows me through the day, and then it really settles in when I get home. Even things that used to feel gentle or grounding, like being in the garden or doing watercolors, feel far away now, like I can see them but can’t quite get myself back to the person who enjoyed them.\n\nSo yes, I guess I do feel sad most of the time, but it’s not always sharp sadness. It’s more like a dull heaviness, or like everything has had the color turned down.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been a little over two years now… about two years and maybe two months, if I’m counting right.\n\nIt started around the time my husband stopped working outside the home, and our whole household routine changed. I don’t want to make it sound like it was one single day and then suddenly everything was different, because it wasn’t that dramatic. It was more gradual than that. But when I look back and try to figure out when I stopped feeling like myself, that’s the period I keep coming back to.\n\nAt first I thought it was just an adjustment, you know, like our schedules were different and the rhythm at home changed, and I figured I’d get used to it. But then months went by, and then a year, and it just sort of stayed. And now it’s been long enough that I can’t really explain it away as a busy season anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms stayed about the same over the past two years, or have they gotten better or worse at times?\n\nPatient: It’s been pretty consistently there, I think. I mean, I can have better days or worse days, but when I step back and look at the whole stretch of time, it’s been going on for about two years and two months now.\n\nIt started around when my husband stopped working outside the home and our household routine changed, and since then it hasn’t really fully lifted. At first I kept thinking, “Okay, this is just the adjustment period, things will settle,” but it just sort of became the new normal. There have been moments where I manage better, but I wouldn’t say it went away. It’s more like it’s been this steady background thing since then.\n\nDoctor: Have there been any periods over the past two years where your symptoms significantly improved or worsened?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it as significantly better or worse, no. It’s more like there’s been a kind of steady low level to it, if that makes sense.\n\nThere are days where I can function better, or where the day is busy enough that I don’t notice it as much until later, and then there are days where it feels heavier. But I can’t point to a stretch of weeks or months and say, “Oh, that was when it really lifted,” or “that was when it got dramatically worse.” It’s more… the same weather, with little changes in temperature, if that makes sense.\n\nI might be missing something because I’m not great at tracking it day by day, but looking back, I don’t think there were any major periods where it clearly improved or clearly worsened. It’s just been there.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least two months where you felt completely back to your normal self since this started?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. Not for two months at a time.\n\nThere have been little stretches where I’ve thought, “Maybe I’m doing a bit better,” or I’ve had a few days where things felt more manageable, but I wouldn’t say I ever got completely back to how I used to feel before all of this started. Since my husband stopped working outside the home and our household routine changed, it’s been kind of continuously there in some form.\n\nIt’s been about two years and two months now, and I can’t point to any two-month period where I felt like, “Oh, I’m myself again, this is over.” It just hasn’t really lifted like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how you're feeling over the course of a week or a month?\n\nPatient: Week to week or month to month, I guess I notice little fluctuations, but not a clear pattern. Like, some weeks feel a bit easier and some feel heavier, but when I zoom out it’s really the same overall thing that’s been going on.\n\nIt’s been about two years and two months now, since my husband stopped working outside the home and our whole household routine changed. That’s kind of the point where I can trace it starting. Since then, it hasn’t really been like, “Oh, this month was normal and then this month it came back.” It’s more that it’s been present the whole time, just with small ups and downs depending on the day or week.\n\nDoctor: Do you find pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy, or has that changed for you?\n\nPatient: It’s changed, yes. I mean, I can still do things, and sometimes I can even look like I’m enjoying them, especially at school. With my students, I can smile, I can be animated enough to get through a lesson, and there are moments where their energy pulls me along.\n\nBut underneath that, it’s like there’s this gray, flat feeling that’s just… there. And when I get home, it’s harder to access the parts of me that used to feel genuinely interested in things. I used to really enjoy my garden, noticing what was coming up, planning little changes, even just being outside with it. And I used to like doing watercolors, not in any serious way, just for myself. Now those things feel kind of far away, like I know they’re mine and I know I used to like them, but I don’t feel connected to them in the same way.\n\nI wouldn’t say I never feel anything pleasant, but the pleasure feels muted. Like I’m going through the motions more than actually feeling absorbed or refreshed by it.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods lasting less than two months where you felt completely back to your normal self since this started?\n\nPatient: I guess… maybe for a few days here and there I’ve felt closer to myself, but not completely back to normal, no. Even the better stretches still feel like this has been in the background.\n\nIt’s hard because when I think back, the whole thing kind of blends together. It’s been going on for about two years and two months, starting around when my husband stopped working outside the home and our household routine changed. Since then, I can’t really identify even a shorter stretch where I’d say I was fully myself again. Maybe “less bad” at times, but not back to normal.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where your symptoms got significantly worse for a while, and then improved, only to come back again?\n\nPatient: Not really in a clear “worse, better, then came back” kind of way. I mean, I’ve had days or maybe a week here and there where things felt heavier, and then a little lighter again, but I wouldn’t describe it as separate episodes.\n\nIt’s more like it’s been one long stretch. About two years and two months now, really since my husband stopped working outside the home and our household routine changed. That was when I first started noticing that something had shifted, and since then it’s sort of just stayed present. The intensity moves around a bit, but it hasn’t felt like it fully improved and then returned. It’s been more continuous than that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed if your symptoms tend to worsen or improve at specific times of the year?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really noticed a clear seasonal pattern, no. I’ve wondered about it a little, like whether winter makes it worse because it’s darker and the school year feels more tiring then, but when I really think about it, I can’t honestly say it lifts in the spring or summer in any dramatic way.\n\nThere are times of year that are more stressful just because of school schedules or holidays or family routines, and that can color things, but it doesn’t feel like the symptoms themselves are tied to a season. It’s more like they’re there in the background regardless, and the season just changes what’s going on around them. So… no, I don’t think I’ve noticed a specific time of year when it reliably gets better or worse.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant improvement in your symptoms for at least two weeks at any point since they started?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no—not in a way that I’d call significant improvement for two full weeks. Since my husband stopped working outside the home and our household routine changed, it’s been kind of continuously there in some form.\n\nIt’s been about two years and two months now, and I can’t point to any two-month period where I felt like, “Oh, I’m myself again, this is over.” It just hasn’t really lifted like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how you're feeling over the course of a week or a month?\n\nPatient: Week to week or month to month, I guess I notice little fluctuations, but not a clear pattern. Like, some weeks feel a bit easier and some feel heavier, but when I zoom out it’s really the same overall thing that’s been going on.\n\nIt’s been about two years and two months now, since my husband stopped working outside the home and our whole household routine changed. That’s kind of the point where I can trace it starting. Since then, it hasn’t really been like, “Oh, this month was normal and then this month it came back.” It’s more that it’s been present the whole time, just with small ups and downs depending on the day or week.\n\nDoctor: Do you find pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy, or has that changed for you?\n\nPatient: It’s changed, yes. I mean, I can still do things, and sometimes I can even look like I’m enjoying them, especially at school. With my students, I can smile, I can be animated enough to get through a lesson, and there are moments where their energy pulls me along.\n\nBut underneath that, it’s like there’s this gray, flat feeling that’s just… there. And when I get home, it’s harder to access the parts of me that used to feel genuinely interested in things. I used to really enjoy my garden, noticing what was coming up, planning little changes, even just being outside with it. And I used to like doing watercolors, not in any serious way, just for myself. Now those things feel kind of far away, like I know they’re mine and I know I used to like them, but I don’t feel connected to them in the same way.\n\nI wouldn’t say I never feel anything pleasant, but the pleasure feels muted. Like I’m going through the motions more than actually feeling absorbed or refreshed by it.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods lasting less than two months where you felt completely back to your normal self since this started?\n\nPatient: I guess… maybe for a few days here and there I’ve felt closer to myself, but not completely back to normal, no. Even the better stretches still feel like this has been in the background.\n\nIt’s hard because when I think back, the whole thing kind of blends together. It’s been going on for about two years and two months, starting around when my husband stopped working outside the home and our household routine changed. Since then, I can’t really identify even a shorter stretch where I’d say I was fully myself again. Maybe “less bad” at times, but not back to normal.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where your symptoms got significantly worse for a while, and then improved, only to come back again?\n\nPatient: Not really in a clear “worse, better, then came back” kind of way. I mean, I’ve had days or maybe a week here and there where things felt heavier, and then a little lighter again, but I wouldn’t describe it as separate episodes.\n\nIt’s more like it’s been one long stretch. About two years and two months now, really since my husband stopped working outside the home and our household routine changed. That was when I first started noticing that something had shifted, and since then it’s sort of just stayed present. The intensity moves around a bit, but it hasn’t felt like it fully improved and then returned. It’s been more continuous than that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed if your symptoms tend to worsen or improve at specific times of the year?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really noticed a clear seasonal pattern, no. I’ve wondered about it a little, like whether winter makes it worse because it’s darker and the school year feels more tiring then, but when I really think about it, I can’t honestly say it lifts in the spring or summer in any dramatic way.\n\nThere are times of year that are more stressful just because of school schedules or holidays or family routines, and that can color things, but it doesn’t feel like the symptoms themselves are tied to a season. It’s more like they’re there in the background regardless, and the season just changes what’s going on around them. It’s more like it’s stayed in the background the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed if anything specific seems to trigger when you're feeling more or less tired and sad?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure there’s one single trigger, honestly. Since then, I can’t really identify even a shorter stretch where I’d say I was fully myself again. Maybe “less bad” at times, but not back to normal.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where your symptoms got significantly worse for a while, and then improved, only to come back again?\n\nPatient: Not really in a clear “worse, better, then came back” kind of way. I mean, I’ve had days or maybe a week here and there where things felt heavier, and then a little lighter again, but I wouldn’t describe it as separate episodes.\n\nIt’s more like it’s been one long stretch. About two years and two months now, really since my husband stopped working outside the home and our household routine changed. That was when I first started noticing that something had shifted, and since then it’s sort of just stayed present. The intensity moves around a bit, but it hasn’t felt like it fully improved and then returned. It’s been more continuous than that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed if your symptoms tend to worsen or improve at specific times of the year?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really noticed a clear seasonal pattern, no. I’ve wondered about it a little, like whether winter makes it worse because it’s darker and the school year feels more tiring then, but when I really think about it, I can’t honestly say it lifts in the spring or summer in any dramatic way.\n\nThere are times of year that are more stressful just because of school schedules or holidays or family routines, and that can color things, but it doesn’t feel like the symptoms themselves are tied to a season. It’s more like they’re there in the background regardless, and the season just changes what’s going on around them. So… no, I don’t think I’ve noticed a specific time of year when it reliably gets better or worse.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant improvement in your symptoms for at least two weeks at any point since they started?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no—not in a way that I’d call significant improvement for two full weeks. There have been better days, and maybe little stretches where I felt like I was functioning a bit more normally, but when I look at the whole picture, it’s really been going on continuously for about two years and two months.\n\nIt started around the time my husband stopped working outside the home and our household routine changed, and since then I haven’t really had a solid couple of weeks where I felt like, “Oh, I’m back to myself.” It’s more like it fades into the background a little sometimes, but it doesn’t really lift.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods where you felt completely back to your normal self for at least two weeks at a time, but then the symptoms returned?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve had that—at least not for two full weeks where I felt completely back to normal and then it returned. It’s more that this has just been going on for about two years and two months now.\n\nI can pretty clearly connect the start of it to when my husband stopped working outside the home and our household routine changed. Since then, things have maybe shifted a little day to day, but I wouldn’t say there was a stretch where I was fully myself again for a couple of weeks. It’s felt more continuous than that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods where you felt completely better for at least two months, but then the symptoms came back?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think there’s been anything like two months where I felt completely better and then it came back. I mean, when I think back over it, it’s been more continuous than that.\n\nIt’s been going on for about two years and two months now, since around when my husband stopped working outside the home and our household routine changed. There are days or little stretches where it feels less front-and-center, maybe because I’m busy or things at home are a little smoother, but I wouldn’t say I was completely better for two months. It’s more like it’s stayed in the background the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed if anything specific seems to trigger when you're feeling more or less tired and sad?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure there’s one single trigger, honestly. The tiredness is kind of there most of the time, and that’s actually a big part of why I came in. It’s not just the normal “I’m a teacher and the day was long” kind of tired, because I know what that feels like. This is more like… even if I sleep through the night, I wake up and I’m already thinking about how much effort it’s going to take to get through the day.\n\nIt tends to really hit me when I get home and still have grading to do, or I need to make dinner, or get things ready for the next morning. Those are the times when I notice it most, because I’m just pushing myself through each thing. It’s not that anything dramatic has to happen first—it’s more like ordinary responsibilities feel heavier than they should.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods where you felt completely better for less than two months, but then the symptoms came back?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a way where I felt completely better and then it clearly came back. I know I’ve mentioned the timing before, but it really has felt like this ongoing thing for about two years and two months now, starting around when my husband stopped working outside the home and our whole routine shifted.\n\nThere have been days, or maybe even a little stretch here and there, where things felt more manageable, but I wouldn’t describe it as being fully back to myself—even for a shorter period. I know I’ve mentioned the timing before, but it really has felt like this ongoing thing for about two years and two months now, starting around when my husband stopped working outside the home and our whole routine shifted.\n\nThere have been days, or maybe even a little stretch here and there, where things felt more manageable, but I wouldn’t describe it as being fully back to myself—even for a shorter period. It’s more like the intensity changes, but it hasn’t really gone away and then returned.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a clear period where you felt depressed, followed by a period where you felt completely normal, and then another period where you felt depressed again?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it’s been like separate episodes like that. I’m trying to think back carefully, because I know that’s what you’re asking—like one clear depressed stretch, then feeling completely normal, and then another depressed stretch—but it hasn’t really had those clean breaks.\n\nIt’s been going on for about two years and two months, since around the time my husband stopped working outside the home and our household routine changed. Since then it’s felt more like one long, continuous thing, even if some days are easier than others. I can have a day where I’m distracted or functioning better, but I wouldn’t say I went back to completely normal and then got depressed again.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever had a period of at least two weeks where you felt completely free of depressive symptoms since this started?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 35 / elementary_or_middle_school_teacher / graduate\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Duration\nmanifestation: \"This has been going on for about two years and two months, since my husband stopped working outside the home and our household routine changed.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I don’t think I’ve had a full two-week stretch where I felt completely free of it. I mean, there have definitely been individual days where I’m more functional or I don’t notice it as much because I’m busy, but if I’m being honest, I wouldn’t call that being completely free of the symptoms.

It’s been pretty continuous for about two years and two months now, really since my husband stopped working outside the home and our household routine changed. The level of it shifts a little, but I don’t remember a solid two weeks where I felt like, “Oh, I’m back to normal, this is gone.”

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.1, F33.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I’m just… tired all the time. And I know that sounds vague, because I’m a teacher, so of course I’m tired at the end of the day, everyone at school is tired. But this feels different. It’s not just “long day with the kids” tired.\n\nEven when I get a full night’s sleep, I wake up and I already feel like I’m pushing through mud. Then after school I still have grading, lesson prep, dinner, getting things ready for the next morning, and it all feels like I have to force myself through every single step. I can do it, mostly, but it takes so much effort. I guess I’m here because it’s been wearing me down, and I don’t know if this is something I’m supposed to just manage or if something’s wrong.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're still enjoying the things you used to enjoy, like teaching or activities outside of school?\n\nPatient: I still enjoy some of it, I think… or at least I care about it. I still show up for my students, and I’m not checked out in the classroom exactly. I can still have moments where a kid finally understands something, or says something funny, and I feel that little spark of why I became a teacher. So it’s not like everything is completely flat.\n\nBut I’ve definitely pulled back from the parts of teaching that used to make me feel more like myself. I used to mentor newer teachers, help with extra projects, make these more creative classroom activities just because I wanted to. Lately I’m doing what needs to be done, but not much beyond that. If someone asks me to join a committee or help plan something, my first thought is just, “I can’t add one more thing.”\n\nOutside of school it’s kind of the same. I still do what I need to for my family, but I don’t really say yes to social things much anymore. I used to meet friends after work or do little creative routines at home that made my life feel fuller, and now I mostly just… don’t. I tell myself I’m too busy or too tired, and sometimes that’s true, but I know I’ve been withdrawing. It feels like I’m still functioning, but my life has gotten smaller.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most days, I think. I wouldn’t say I’m crying all the time or obviously miserable from the outside, because I’m usually still able to smile for my students and get through the lessons and be “on” when I need to be. In the classroom, I can sort of step into that role, and I don’t want the kids to feel it from me.\n\nBut underneath that, there’s this gray, flat feeling that’s just… there. It follows me through the day, and then it really settles in when I get home. Even things that used to feel gentle or grounding, like being in the garden or doing watercolors, feel far away now, like I can see them but can’t quite get myself back to the person who enjoyed them.\n\nSo yes, I guess I do feel sad most of the time, but it’s not always sharp sadness. It’s more like a dull heaviness, or like everything has had the color turned down.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been a little over two years now… about two years and maybe two months, if I’m counting right.\n\nIt started around the time my husband stopped working outside the home, and our whole household routine changed. I don’t want to make it sound like it was one single day and then suddenly everything was different, because it wasn’t that dramatic. It was more gradual than that. But when I look back and try to figure out when I stopped feeling like myself, that’s the period I keep coming back to.\n\nAt first I thought it was just an adjustment, you know, like our schedules were different and the rhythm at home changed, and I figured I’d get used to it. But then months went by, and then a year, and it just sort of stayed. And now it’s been long enough that I can’t really explain it away as a busy season anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms stayed about the same over the past two years, or have they gotten better or worse at times?\n\nPatient: It’s been pretty consistently there, I think. I mean, I can have better days or worse days, but when I step back and look at the whole stretch of time, it’s been going on for about two years and two months now.\n\nIt started around when my husband stopped working outside the home and our household routine changed, and since then it hasn’t really fully lifted. At first I kept thinking, “Okay, this is just the adjustment period, things will settle,” but it just sort of became the new normal. There have been moments where I manage better, but I wouldn’t say it went away. It’s more like it’s been this steady background thing since then.\n\nDoctor: Have there been any periods over the past two years where your symptoms significantly improved or worsened?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it as significantly better or worse, no. It’s more like there’s been a kind of steady low level to it, if that makes sense.\n\nThere are days where I can function better, or where the day is busy enough that I don’t notice it as much until later, and then there are days where it feels heavier. But I can’t point to a stretch of weeks or months and say, “Oh, that was when it really lifted,” or “that was when it got dramatically worse.” It’s more… the same weather, with little changes in temperature, if that makes sense.\n\nI might be missing something because I’m not great at tracking it day by day, but looking back, I don’t think there were any major periods where it clearly improved or clearly worsened. It’s just been there.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least two months where you felt completely back to your normal self since this started?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. Not for two months at a time.\n\nThere have been little stretches where I’ve thought, “Maybe I’m doing a bit better,” or I’ve had a few days where things felt more manageable, but I wouldn’t say I ever got completely back to how I used to feel before all of this started. Since my husband stopped working outside the home and our household routine changed, it’s been kind of continuously there in some form.\n\nIt’s been about two years and two months now, and I can’t point to any two-month period where I felt like, “Oh, I’m myself again, this is over.” It just hasn’t really lifted like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how you're feeling over the course of a week or a month?\n\nPatient: Week to week or month to month, I guess I notice little fluctuations, but not a clear pattern. Like, some weeks feel a bit easier and some feel heavier, but when I zoom out it’s really the same overall thing that’s been going on.\n\nIt’s been about two years and two months now, since my husband stopped working outside the home and our whole household routine changed. That’s kind of the point where I can trace it starting. Since then, it hasn’t really been like, “Oh, this month was normal and then this month it came back.” It’s more that it’s been present the whole time, just with small ups and downs depending on the day or week.\n\nDoctor: Do you find pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy, or has that changed for you?\n\nPatient: It’s changed, yes. I mean, I can still do things, and sometimes I can even look like I’m enjoying them, especially at school. With my students, I can smile, I can be animated enough to get through a lesson, and there are moments where their energy pulls me along.\n\nBut underneath that, it’s like there’s this gray, flat feeling that’s just… there. And when I get home, it’s harder to access the parts of me that used to feel genuinely interested in things. I used to really enjoy my garden, noticing what was coming up, planning little changes, even just being outside with it. And I used to like doing watercolors, not in any serious way, just for myself. Now those things feel kind of far away, like I know they’re mine and I know I used to like them, but I don’t feel connected to them in the same way.\n\nI wouldn’t say I never feel anything pleasant, but the pleasure feels muted. Like I’m going through the motions more than actually feeling absorbed or refreshed by it.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods lasting less than two months where you felt completely back to your normal self since this started?\n\nPatient: I guess… maybe for a few days here and there I’ve felt closer to myself, but not completely back to normal, no. Even the better stretches still feel like this has been in the background.\n\nIt’s hard because when I think back, the whole thing kind of blends together. It’s been going on for about two years and two months, starting around when my husband stopped working outside the home and our household routine changed. Since then, I can’t really identify even a shorter stretch where I’d say I was fully myself again. Maybe “less bad” at times, but not back to normal.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where your symptoms got significantly worse for a while, and then improved, only to come back again?\n\nPatient: Not really in a clear “worse, better, then came back” kind of way. I mean, I’ve had days or maybe a week here and there where things felt heavier, and then a little lighter again, but I wouldn’t describe it as separate episodes.\n\nIt’s more like it’s been one long stretch. About two years and two months now, really since my husband stopped working outside the home and our household routine changed. That was when I first started noticing that something had shifted, and since then it’s sort of just stayed present. The intensity moves around a bit, but it hasn’t felt like it fully improved and then returned. It’s been more continuous than that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed if your symptoms tend to worsen or improve at specific times of the year?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really noticed a clear seasonal pattern, no. I’ve wondered about it a little, like whether winter makes it worse because it’s darker and the school year feels more tiring then, but when I really think about it, I can’t honestly say it lifts in the spring or summer in any dramatic way.\n\nThere are times of year that are more stressful just because of school schedules or holidays or family routines, and that can color things, but it doesn’t feel like the symptoms themselves are tied to a season. It’s more like they’re there in the background regardless, and the season just changes what’s going on around them. So… no, I don’t think I’ve noticed a specific time of year when it reliably gets better or worse.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant improvement in your symptoms for at least two weeks at any point since they started?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no—not in a way that I’d call significant improvement for two full weeks. There have been better days, and maybe little stretches where I felt like I was functioning a bit more normally, but when I look at the whole picture, it’s really been going on continuously for about two years and two months.\n\nIt started around the time my husband stopped working outside the home and our household routine changed, and since then I haven’t really had a solid couple of weeks where I felt like, “Oh, I’m back to myself.” It’s more like it fades into the background a little sometimes, but it doesn’t really lift.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods where you felt completely back to your normal self for at least two weeks at a time, but then the symptoms returned?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve had that—at least not for two full weeks where I felt completely back to normal and then it returned. It’s more that this has just been going on for about two years and two months now.\n\nI can pretty clearly connect the start of it to when my husband stopped working outside the home and our household routine changed. Since then, things have maybe shifted a little day to day, but I wouldn’t say there was a stretch where I was fully myself again for a couple of weeks. It’s felt more continuous than that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods where you felt completely better for at least two months, but then the symptoms came back?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think there’s been anything like two months where I felt completely better and then it came back. I mean, when I think back over it, it’s been more continuous than that.\n\nIt’s been going on for about two years and two months now, since around when my husband stopped working outside the home and our household routine changed. There are days or little stretches where it feels less front-and-center, maybe because I’m busy or things at home are a little smoother, but I wouldn’t say I was completely better for two months. It’s more like it’s stayed in the background the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed if anything specific seems to trigger when you're feeling more or less tired and sad?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure there’s one single trigger, honestly. The tiredness is kind of there most of the time, and that’s actually a big part of why I came in. It’s not just the normal “I’m a teacher and the day was long” kind of tired, because I know what that feels like. This is more like… even if I sleep through the night, I wake up and I’m already thinking about how much effort it’s going to take to get through the day.\n\nIt tends to really hit me when I get home and still have grading to do, or I need to make dinner, or get things ready for the next morning. Those are the times when I notice it most, because I’m just pushing myself through each thing. It’s not that anything dramatic has to happen first—it’s more like ordinary responsibilities feel heavier than they should.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods where you felt completely better for less than two months, but then the symptoms came back?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a way where I felt completely better and then it clearly came back. I know I’ve mentioned the timing before, but it really has felt like this ongoing thing for about two years and two months now, starting around when my husband stopped working outside the home and our whole routine shifted.\n\nThere have been days, or maybe even a little stretch here and there, where things felt more manageable, but I wouldn’t describe it as being fully back to myself—even for a shorter period. It’s more like the intensity changes, but it hasn’t really gone away and then returned.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a clear period where you felt depressed, followed by a period where you felt completely normal, and then another period where you felt depressed again?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it’s been like separate episodes like that. I’m trying to think back carefully, because I know that’s what you’re asking—like one clear depressed stretch, then feeling completely normal, and then another depressed stretch—but it hasn’t really had those clean breaks.\n\nIt’s been going on for about two years and two months, since around the time my husband stopped working outside the home and our household routine changed. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I mean, I’ve had days or maybe a week here and there where things felt heavier, and then a little lighter again, but I wouldn’t describe it as separate episodes.\n\nIt’s more like it’s been one long stretch. About two years and two months now, really since my husband stopped working outside the home and our household routine changed. That was when I first started noticing that something had shifted, and since then it’s sort of just stayed present. The intensity moves around a bit, but it hasn’t felt like it fully improved and then returned. It’s been more continuous than that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed if your symptoms tend to worsen or improve at specific times of the year?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really noticed a clear seasonal pattern, no. I’ve wondered about it a little, like whether winter makes it worse because it’s darker and the school year feels more tiring then, but when I really think about it, I can’t honestly say it lifts in the spring or summer in any dramatic way.\n\nThere are times of year that are more stressful just because of school schedules or holidays or family routines, and that can color things, but it doesn’t feel like the symptoms themselves are tied to a season. It’s more like they’re there in the background regardless, and the season just changes what’s going on around them. So… no, I don’t think I’ve noticed a specific time of year when it reliably gets better or worse.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant improvement in your symptoms for at least two weeks at any point since they started?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no—not in a way that I’d call significant improvement for two full weeks. There have been better days, and maybe little stretches where I felt like I was functioning a bit more normally, but when I look at the whole picture, it’s really been going on continuously for about two years and two months.\n\nIt started around the time my husband stopped working outside the home and our household routine changed, and since then I haven’t really had a solid couple of weeks where I felt like, “Oh, I’m back to myself.” It’s more like it fades into the background a little sometimes, but it doesn’t really lift.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods where you felt completely back to your normal self for at least two weeks at a time, but then the symptoms returned?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve had that—at least not for two full weeks where I felt completely back to normal and then it returned. It’s more that this has just been going on for about two years and two months now.\n\nI can pretty clearly connect the start of it to when my husband stopped working outside the home and our household routine changed. Since then, things have maybe shifted a little day to day, but I wouldn’t say there was a stretch where I was fully myself again for a couple of weeks. It’s felt more continuous than that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods where you felt completely better for at least two months, but then the symptoms came back?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think there’s been anything like two months where I felt completely better and then it came back. I mean, when I think back over it, it’s been more continuous than that.\n\nIt’s been going on for about two years and two months now, since around when my husband stopped working outside the home and our household routine changed. There are days or little stretches where it feels less front-and-center, maybe because I’m busy or things at home are a little smoother, but I wouldn’t say I was completely better for two months. It’s more like it’s stayed in the background the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed if anything specific seems to trigger when you're feeling more or less tired and sad?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure there’s one single trigger, honestly. The tiredness is kind of there most of the time, and that’s actually a big part of why I came in. It’s not just the normal “I’m a teacher and the day was long” kind of tired, because I know what that feels like. This is more like… even if I sleep through the night, I wake up and I’m already thinking about how much effort it’s going to take to get through the day.\n\nIt tends to really hit me when I get home and still have grading to do, or I need to make dinner, or get things ready for the next morning. Those are the times when I notice it most, because I’m just pushing myself through each thing. It’s not that anything dramatic has to happen first—it’s more like ordinary responsibilities feel heavier than they should.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods where you felt completely better for less than two months, but then the symptoms came back?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a way where I felt completely better and then it clearly came back. It’s more like the intensity changes, but it hasn’t really gone away and then returned.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a clear period where you felt depressed, followed by a period where you felt completely normal, and then another period where you felt depressed again?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it’s been like separate episodes like that. I’m trying to think back carefully, because I know that’s what you’re asking—like one clear depressed stretch, then feeling completely normal, and then another depressed stretch—but it hasn’t really had those clean breaks.\n\nIt’s been going on for about two years and two months, since around the time my husband stopped working outside the home and our household routine changed.
